(The attempt bound is no longer deferred: since M6-PG-P5 the store claims an event at most 20 times and dead-letters an expired claim at 20.) | M6-PG-P4, M6-PG-P5 | Pre-launch review, with the binding stage |
| DA-15 | The pinned driver (postgres 3.4.9, the latest release) mishandles a store connection lost under it, and a pool ended during an outage; the PostgreSQL suite reproduces all three defects in processes of their own (M6-PG-24, M6-PG-33). (1) A write it is handed after its connection closed — the ROLLBACK its transaction scope sends for a body that rejects, or a statement or COMMIT from a body still running — goes to a null socket: an uncaught `TypeError` (`Cannot read properties of null (reading 'write')`), and the process exits. (2) A connection that closes in the same turn as a write it was handed keeps a stale write timer, so that slot's start-up message is never sent and every later use of it waits out connect_timeout (in M6-PG-P4, 2 of 12 suite runs under load, through a server-ended idle session handed a call before its close was seen); which slot, and whether, cannot be seen from outside the driver. (3) Found in M6-PG-P6: `reserve()` on a pool whose connection is not open queues its request and opens the connection; when that attempt fails the request stays queued, so the pool's close handling reconnects for it, and `end()` does not stop a reconnect already scheduled (`connect()` clears the terminated flag). A pool ended during an outage therefore opens an idle session nobody owns once the server is back, which lives until the client's idle timeout (20 s for the runtime principal) — or, with none, indefinitely. The first M6-PG-P6 kernel met it: the suite's outages left enough such sessions to exhaust the disposable cluster's 20 connections. Upstream, reviewed 2026-09-15 from official sources only: 3.4.9 is the latest release and master is two unrelated commits ahead; (1) is open as issues #1208, #1154 and #1199, with candidate fixes in open PRs #1209 and #1168 and in #1176, closed unmerged; (2) has no report; (3) was found after that review and has not been checked against upstream. No released version is assumed to fix any of them. **Contained, dependency unchanged (M6-PG-P5, completed in M6-PG-P6):** the store reaches the driver only through the transaction kernel of `createSupervisedPgClient` (`server/persistence/supervisedPgClient.ts`; DA-19, DA-20). (1) is never reached: a transaction's gate shuts synchronously inside the driver's close handling and is checked at each statement's hand-off to its connection, so nothing is handed to a closed connection (M6-PG-25, -26, -30, -31, -32). (2): a pool of one connection whose connection closed, or whose transaction stopped trusting it, is retired and never reused, and the next call opens a fresh pool within its deadline (M6-PG-25, -26). (3): a new pool's connection is opened by one plain statement through the same gate — which the driver hands to the connection it opens, never to its queue — and only an opened pool is reserved, so a refused attempt leaves nothing queued and an ended pool no refused request to reconnect for (M6-PG-33, beside the driver's own witness). Nothing is retried, failures before COMMIT stay `unavailable`, an ambiguous COMMIT stays indeterminate, and `end()` closes whatever still runs after a 5 s grace. Residual: each call that meets a poisoned slot fails, after connect_timeout, before its pool is retired; a connection partitioned without a close is found only by TCP keepalive (the driver's default, 60 s) or the server's own timeouts, each call on it ending at its deadline as `unavailable` until then; the driver retries at once, without end, a connection attempt whose socket closes without an error while it connects — a new pool's opening statement waits through those retries until the call's deadline answers it, and if the call is abandoned, or `end()`'s grace runs out, during them, the next retry can still open one session after its pool was ended, which the idle timeout closes; a transaction run through the driver directly, not through the kernel, is not contained; and a retired pool whose end fails is not reported — nothing is counted until a bounded telemetry sink exists | M6-PG-P4 | Contained (M6-PG-P6). Before binding: the store composed only through `createSupervisedPgClient`, and no other transaction caller. Re-read on any driver change: M6-PG-24 and M6-PG-33 fail once a defect is gone |
| DA-16 | Delivered and dead were terminal for the adapter's statements, not for the table: the runtime role's column grants and its RLS policy (`using (true)`) would have let other SQL run as that role — a mutator's, say — move a delivered or dead event back to pending, or insert one already claimed. **Closed in M6-PG-P5:** a trigger on `tmpos_internal.outbox_event` (migration 006, `ENABLE ALWAYS`) admits inserts only as pending at attempt 0 and updates only from pending to claimed once due, and from claimed to claimed once expired or to pending, delivered or dead — a claim counting exactly one attempt and no update rewriting the envelope; delivered and dead are frozen. It holds for every writer, `session_replication_role = replica` included (proved by the PostgreSQL suite's M6-PG-21, as the owner and through the adapter). Only disabling or dropping the trigger — the table's owner or a superuser — bypasses it, never the runtime role. Since M6-PG-P6 the runtime role's own SQL, a mutator's included, reaches the table only through migration 006's routines, which run as the owner and pass the same trigger (DA-18) | M6-PG-P4 review | Closed (M6-PG-P5) |
| DA-17 | The runtime client (`server/platform-identity/db.ts`) set no `onnotice`, so postgres.js would print any server NOTICE — message, detail and location — to stdout. The adapter raises none, but a mutator or a trigger could; the PostgreSQL suite overrode `onnotice`, so it did not exercise the production-shaped client. **Closed in M6-PG-P5:** the shared client options of both principals carry `discardNotice`, which drops every notice and counts none until a bounded telemetry sink exists; the suite's store clients now use those options unchanged, M6-PG-27 proves through a real server that a notice raised on them reaches no output while the driver's default prints it, and a unit test pins the handler on both constructions | M6-PG-P4 review | Closed (M6-PG-P5) |
| DA-18 | The transition guard (DA-16) fixed the shape of the delivery state machine, not who drives it or when, and nothing shaped `idempotency_record` at all. The runtime role held column UPDATE on both tables under a policy admitting every row, so SQL running as that role — a mutator's, say — could release a live claim and claim it again, settle an event another instance held, give an event or a retry any finite due time and a claim any finite lease; and on a record it could shut out an in-flight operation, hand the scope to a different request, park it until the year 9999, and **cause the same business effect twice** — moving a completed record's times into the past made the next request find it absent (acquisition checks retention before fingerprint and replay, and re-leasing cleared the stored response). The CHECK constraints admitted every one of these values, and the claim token and lease that fence the ports were readable by that role. **Closed in M6-PG-P6** (migration 006, corrected in place — applied to no managed database): `tmpos_app` holds no privilege on either table and none on the clock — only USAGE on `tmpos_internal` and EXECUTE on nine fixed routines (`m6_idempotency_acquire`, `m6_idempotency_complete`, `m6_command_fence`, `m6_command_enqueue`, `m6_outbox_claim`, `m6_outbox_acknowledge`, `m6_outbox_retry`, `m6_outbox_dead_letter`, `m6_store_probe`). Each is `SECURITY DEFINER`, owned by the principal that applies 006 (no role is created), pinned to `pg_catalog, pg_temp`, fixed SQL with no dynamic statement or caller-chosen identifier, strictly validated (a malformed argument raises 22023, an unreadable clock 55000, each with one fixed message), revoked from PUBLIC and granted to `tmpos_app` alone. Every decision locks its row, then reads the store's clock: a record is created bounded (lease 1 ms–24 h, retention that lease to 7 days), its lease replaced only once expired, its response stored only under exactly the presented lease within retention and never replaced, and a completed record replayed to its own fingerprint and in conflict with any other until the retention boundary, exactly as the port contract says; an event is enqueued only under a held lease, claimed only when due or expired and never under the token that held the expired claim (the guard now refuses that too), settled only under exactly the presented token (`claim_lost` otherwise, changing nothing), rescheduled within 15 min, and claimed at most 20 times, a cap fixed in the claim routine that no argument reaches. RLS stays enabled with no policy, so a stray grant reaches no row, and the transition guard stays. Section 6 now refuses any direct grant in the schema beyond `tmpos_app`'s USAGE and routine EXECUTE (grant option included), any function in it that is not one of 006's eleven exactly as defined (definer attribute, owner, pinned path), and `tmpos_app`, `tmpos_audit_writer`, `anon` or `authenticated` being a member of the owner. Proved against disposable PostgreSQL by direct SQL as the runtime login (M6-PG-02: every read and write of either table and the clock refused with 42501; M6-PG-29: each routine's lifecycle rules, bounds and stale-caller answers), by the section-6 refusals (M6-PG-28), by the effective-reach matrix (M6-PG-20) and by the three unchanged conformance suites. Residual: the owner and a superuser still bypass everything, and who may become either is G-DBROLE's live check | M6-PG-P5 review | Closed (M6-PG-P6) |
| DA-19 | The store's `ended` flag is set too late to cover a close that lands between statements. `begin()` races the body's scope against the connection's close and returns the race; a close that wins rejects the call but leaves the scope running, and `ended` is set only once that rejection has propagated back to the store, a few microtasks later. A connection lost while a statement is in flight is safe: the driver rejects that statement before it reports the close, and the store's wrapper never settles it. But a close processed after a statement returned and before `ended` is set leaves three writes free to reach the socket the close has nulled: **the body's next statement** — the wrapper's `ended` check still passes, and the driver's close handling never marks the connection terminated, so it writes the statement (`commit` issues its clock read straight after the fence select, for one); **COMMIT**, which the scope sends through the same statement object for a body that resolves; and **ROLLBACK**, which it sends for one that throws — the store's own abort check (`if (signal.aborted) refuse(UNAVAILABLE)`) sits after the last `ended` check and before `committing`, so a deadline expiring there, or any refusal a mutator raises, throws into the scope's catch. Each is DA-15 defect (1): a short payload written from a scheduled callback, uncaught, ending the process. The exposed window is the microtask gap after any statement returned, not only after the last. The crash is not the only outcome there: a slot the supervisor has already retired is `terminated`, and the driver answers `CONNECTION_DESTROYED` instead of writing, which is what a forced reproducer hit (an uncommitted experiment, not a committed case — no regression test covers this window yet); a slot carrying DA-15 defect (2)'s stale write timer drops the write silently and the scope hangs instead. Whether retirement wins the race is timing; the refusal itself is a driver branch. The store's own answer stays fail-closed in every ordering and nothing is replayed — note `committing` is set when the body resolves, one step before COMMIT is written, so an indeterminate answer there is conservative rather than exact — but "liveness only" holds only per call: a crash ends every concurrent call's answer too, and `onclose` is nulled after firing while the same connection object can be reconnected for a queued query, so a late COMMIT or ROLLBACK could land on a fresh session with no transaction open. Latent (it needs pool-queue pressure), and outside the liveness framing. Closing it needs two things. First, no driver-owned continuation: hold a reserved connection and issue BEGIN, the body and COMMIT through the store's own wrapper. Second, a gate that the close itself sets synchronously, before any continuation runs — the reserved connection alone would not do, because `ended` is set too late even for the store's own statements. The driver's `onclose` client option fires synchronously inside its close handling and is a candidate for that gate; whether it suffices is for that stage to prove **Closed in M6-PG-P6.** The store no longer uses `begin()`. Every transaction runs in the kernel of `createSupervisedPgClient` over pools of one connection each, which the kernel builds itself (`max` forced to 1, the close callback its own): BEGIN, the transaction-local settings, every body statement, COMMIT and ROLLBACK are issued on one reserved connection through one dispatcher, and no driver-owned scope sends anything. The gate is set by the close itself: the driver calls a pool's `onclose` synchronously inside its close handling — after rejecting the statement in flight, before anything awaiting it runs — and the kernel shuts that transaction's gate in that call; a statement failure other than a server ERROR on an open connection shuts it too, before its rejection is delivered; and the gate is checked in each statement's own hand-off to its connection (the `handler` the driver calls a microtask after the statement is built), so a statement built before a close is refused there instead of written. ROLLBACK is sent only with the gate open and nothing in flight; otherwise the pool is retired unreleased and the server rolls the transaction back as the session ends. A loss before COMMIT is handed over answers `unavailable`, and a close while the body waits answers at once; once COMMIT is handed over, a lost answer is indeterminate (`transactional_store_outcome_unknown`). Nothing is replayed. A pool is never reused after a close, or after a failure that may have lost its connection, and transactions beyond the pool count wait in the kernel, in order, under their deadline — the driver's queue never holds another transaction's statement, so no closed connection object is reconnected for one. The race is closed by that ordering, not by retirement winning it. Proved in child processes against disposable PostgreSQL, the witness being the driver's own record of every statement it hands a connection and of every close: M6-PG-25 loses the connection after a completed statement — by FIN, reset and server termination through a TCP relay, and by a close delivered synchronously inside the mutator's call — before the next body statement, before a successful body's COMMIT and before a failing body's ROLLBACK, and also between a statement's build and its hand-off: each answer `unavailable`, nothing handed to the closed connection, no COMMIT or ROLLBACK without its own BEGIN, the next call served within its deadline on a fresh connection, and the process exits normally; M6-PG-26 a write queued at the close and the slot that close would poison; M6-PG-30 a COMMIT answer lost (indeterminate, landed once, replays, never repeated); M6-PG-31 the server ending one of several transactions under pool-queue pressure (that call alone fails, the waiting calls commit, no late COMMIT or ROLLBACK on any connection); M6-PG-32 `end()` bounded over a transaction stuck in a long statement; and the kernel's unit suite each ordering against a driver double | M6-PG-P5 review | Closed (M6-PG-P6). Re-read on any driver change |
| DA-20 | The driver's savepoints are outside the store's containment entirely. `begin()` installs its close detection only on the outermost scope, so a nested scope opened by `tx.savepoint(...)` has none — its promise neither resolves nor rejects when the connection is lost — and that scope builds a fresh statement object which never passes through the store's `ended` wrapper, adding `savepoint sN` and `rollback to sN` as further ungated writes. Latent today, and narrower than it first appears: nothing in the tree calls `.savepoint` or `.prepare`, and a mutator cannot reach them — the store passes a mutator its own statement wrapper, a plain function carrying neither method, and the audit executor is likewise a fresh function carrying only `json`. The raw transaction object never leaves the adapter file. So the exposure is adapter-internal: only code inside the store could open a nested scope, and that is the code to keep clear of it. Closing it belongs with DA-19's reserved-connection kernel **Closed in M6-PG-P6, by construction.** The kernel uses no `begin()`, no savepoint helper and no other part of the driver's transaction API, and no savepoint support was added. A mutator is handed only the kernel's scope — a frozen statement function carrying `json` and nothing else, never a reserved connection, a pool or the driver — and the audit executor is built from it. The scope runs a statement only if its text is fixed — a frozen template whose parts are plain strings, since the driver reads the text again when it executes the statement — and begins with SELECT, INSERT, UPDATE, DELETE, WITH or VALUES. Everything else is refused before the driver is asked: transaction control and SAVEPOINT, COPY (whose answer is a live stream), session commands (SET, DO, CALL, LOCK, LISTEN, EXECUTE), text that does not begin with a word (a comment, a semicolon or a parenthesis first), text that could still change, a value that is neither plain nor a JSON parameter the scope built, a second statement while one is in flight, and any statement once the call's deadline has passed. A statement is one command, so the kernel's own BEGIN, COMMIT and ROLLBACK are the only transaction control that reaches the server; an answer that is not rows closes the gate, and rows no longer carry the connection's cancel key. The unit suite refuses each form (28 refusals, none reaching the connection) and finds no `savepoint`, `begin`, `unsafe`, `reserve` or `release` on the scope; the store's static test finds no `.begin(`, `savepoint`, `.reserve(`, `.release(` or `prepare transaction` in the adapter. Not contained by M6-PG-P6: session state a mutator leaves through a function in a query — a setting changed with `set_config(..., false)`, a session advisory lock — outlived its transaction on the reused connection (DA-21, closed in M6-PG-P7) | M6-PG-P5 review | Closed (M6-PG-P6) |
| DA-21 | A transaction ended; its session did not. The M6-PG-P6 kernel released a connection to its pool as soon as COMMIT or ROLLBACK was acknowledged, so whatever the body left in the session reached the next transaction on that connection, for whichever caller it served: a setting changed for the session (`set_config(..., false)`, `app.tenant_id` and the other keys migration 005's policies read included, or `default_transaction_read_only`), a role, a session advisory lock, LISTEN, a temporary table, a held cursor, a prepared statement, sequence state. A SELECT reaches all of them through a function, so no refusal of statement text can. **Closed in M6-PG-P7:** a connection returns to its pool only when its transaction's outcome is known (COMMIT or ROLLBACK acknowledged), nothing is in flight, the close gate is open, its caller has not aborted, the client is not ending, and `DISCARD ALL` — sent by the kernel through the same gate and dispatcher, never through a mutator's scope — has been acknowledged; only then is it released, and only then can a caller waiting for the pool have it. Anything else retires the pool. A mutator's statement function stops working once its body settles, so one kept past the body — called from a timer, say — is refused instead of reaching the connection after its reset, or inside another caller's transaction. `DISCARD ALL` is PostgreSQL's whole-session reset (CLOSE ALL, SET SESSION AUTHORIZATION DEFAULT, RESET ALL, DEALLOCATE ALL, UNLISTEN \*, `pg_advisory_unlock_all()`, DISCARD PLANS, TEMP and SEQUENCES) and fails with 25001 inside a transaction block, so a reset that succeeds also shows the session left its transaction. Because it deallocates prepared statements, the kernel builds every pool with the driver's `prepare` forced off, whatever it is given: the driver then names no statement and keeps none, so nothing it holds goes stale, and its automatic re-send of a statement the server reports missing or changed — made outside the kernel's gate, and only for a prepared statement — cannot occur (on disposable PostgreSQL with `prepare` on, the driver re-sent a statement after `DISCARD ALL`). A COMMIT the server answers ROLLBACK — as it does for a block that an error the body caught left aborted — is `unavailable`, never the body's value, and the reset still follows, since the transaction did end. The answer is fixed before the reset: a reset that fails, a close right behind the COMMIT or ROLLBACK answer, during the reset or between its build and hand-off, or an abort while it runs retires the pool and changes nothing about the answer — a close or abort then gives it at once; an indeterminate COMMIT is never reset, and nothing is retried. An abort now retires the pool at once in every phase but COMMIT's (whose own outcome still answers): after cancelling a body statement in flight, instead of rolling back and reusing the connection, so a body, statement, ROLLBACK or reset that never finishes holds no pool and a waiting caller is served by a fresh one. Proved on disposable PostgreSQL: M6-PG-34 (each state above cleared on the same backend, the advisory lock free to another session the moment the call answers — and the same principal, statements and session through the driver alone, with no reset, keeping every one); M6-PG-35 (200 reset-and-reuse cycles on one session with parameterized statements, no prepared statement on the session, each statement taken by the driver exactly once, `prepare: true` forced off; a caller queued for the pool begins only after the reset, on the same session, with the previous session lock gone); M6-PG-36 in child processes (a close right behind the answer, with `DISCARD ALL` unanswered, and between its build and hand-off, and an abort while it stalls, each for a committing and a fenced command: the answer stands and comes promptly, the effects exist once and replay, nothing is taken on the closed connection, the caller queued behind it is served on a fresh one, and the process exits normally); M6-PG-37 (a body that catches its own statement error is `unavailable`, and a statement function kept past its transaction is refused while the next transaction on that session finds no setting); and the kernel's unit suite for each ordering. Residual: `DISCARD ALL` does not restore `random()`'s seed (`setseed()`), and a custom setting once defined keeps its name, reading `''` rather than unset — migration 005's policies read `nullif(current_setting(...), '')`, and no migration or the store uses `random()`; a temporary replication slot needs the REPLICATION attribute, which the runtime login lacks; a failed reset retires its pool uncounted, like every retirement, until a bounded telemetry sink exists (G-OBSERVE). Precondition: a pool of one is one server session only over a direct or session-mode connection — behind a transaction-mode pooler the reset, like COMMIT itself, could reach another backend. At M6-PG-P7 that was a stated condition nothing enforced, so DA-21 was closed in the kernel only conditionally, for direct and session-mode endpoints. **M6-PG-P7-R1 enforces it in production composition:** the transaction root classifies `APP_DATABASE_URL` before any DNS, socket, TLS or SQL and composes the store only over the direct database host or the shared session pooler on port 5432; the shared and dedicated transaction poolers (6543) and every unknown, ambiguous or malformed endpoint are refused with bounded codes, so a transaction-mode endpoint is structurally refused for this adapter (`server/platform-identity/databaseEndpoint.ts`, bound to the frozen migration executor's endpoint grammar by a parity suite). The kernel is built by the database-client boundary with every routing value and the credential as explicit options under the shared transport policy, so the driver parses no connection string and no ambient `PGHOST`, `PGPORT`, `PGUSER`, `PGPASSWORD` or `PGDATABASE` stands in; the driver still reads its non-routing environment defaults (`PGAPPNAME`, `PGTARGETSESSIONATTRS`, `PGMAX_LIFETIME` and the like), and the legacy runtime client `getRuntimeDb()` — which no deployable entry composes — reads `APP_DATABASE_URL` without this classification. `APP_DATABASE_URL` is the runtime role's future secret, never `SUPABASE_DATABASE_URL`, provisioned only after G-DBROLE creates the runtime login and its endpoint; no secret was created. Assurance items, not blockers: the kept-body-scope refusal has a red run in the kernel's unit suite but none on disposable PostgreSQL, and session authorization has no test of its own (the runtime login cannot switch to another role, and `DISCARD ALL` resets it). An abort while COMMIT is on its way still waits for COMMIT's own outcome, which a partitioned connection delays until TCP keepalive or `end()` | M6-PG-P6 review | Closed (M6-PG-P7; precondition enforced in production composition, M6-PG-P7-R1). Re-read on any driver change |

On a definitive `step_up_required` for Suspend or Reactivate, the page discards the confirmation's reason and key and opens a dialog that names the tenant and the action and says nothing was changed. "Sign in again" runs the provider's own in-page sign-in (password or Google, then the TOTP second factor) and sends the new token to the login route's reauthentication mode; the server alone decides that the same administrator signed in, with unchanged standing (the presented session's security version equals the new admission's), and rotates the session to the new provider-verified authentication time, and the old cookie cannot borrow it. The page then reads the directory again, returns to the same tenant, and offers the action only if it still applies, as a new intent: a newly typed reason, the re-read version and a new idempotency key. A different administrator, a failed or cancelled sign-in, or a provider outage leaves the tenant unchanged, and an ended session goes to the console sign-in with nothing resumed. Evidence: `server/runtime/sessions.test.ts` reauthentication cases over real sockets (the same administrator rotates; a different administrator, no or an expired session, a bad header and a stale security version are refused with nothing created or revoked; a store outage is the bounded 503; an admission denial; a refreshed token without a new sign-in is refused by the login auth-age policy); `tests/db/productionEntry.integration.test.mjs` M6EP-21 (`step_up_required`, then a same-administrator reauthentication and a newly confirmed status command; the old cookie refused), M6EP-22 (a different administrator refused, the tenant unchanged) and M6EP-23 (a refreshed token refused); the recent-sign-in cases in `src/backend-control-plane/console/adminSessionClient.test.ts`; the step-up cases in `src/backend-control-plane/console/TenantManagement.test.tsx`; and the step-up scenarios of `tests/browser/admin-console.browser.test.mjs` on the production build (a keyboard-only journey with a new reason and key; a different administrator cannot resume; refusal and Cancel leave the tenant unchanged; the dialog reflows at 320 CSS px / 400 % zoom). | DA-41 | **Only a TENANT lifecycle has a monotonic witness** *(closed in code M5-TENANT-SESSION-P1)*. The digest is a pure function of current standing plus `tenant.access_version`, which never returns to a value it has held. A MEMBERSHIP toggled active → inactive → active, and a STORE suspended and reactivated, move nothing monotonic: 007 filters both out while they are down, and the answer afterwards is byte-identical to the answer before. A session idle across such an episode would therefore be admitted on its old witness — the exact revival the access version closes for tenants. Unreachable today: no shipped command changes a membership's status or a store's, and the episode must also fit inside the session's 30-minute idle window. Asserted as a characterization test (M5A-05b: a store session idle across the episode is served again on its old witness) so it cannot be forgotten. **Closed in code by migration 016 (M5-TENANT-SESSION-P1, [ADR-31](./10-architecture-decision-records.md)).** A trigger owns `store.access_version` and `tenant.access_version`. It moves each by one on every UPDATE that changes the row's status or, for a store, its `tenant_id`, whoever writes it (a raw owner write and a replica's included), refuses any other change to either version, and starts every new store at 1 whatever its INSERT names. It refuses (23514), as a replica too, any UPDATE that would change a tenant's `tenant_id` or a store's `store_id`: a review found that a replica re-key followed by an INSERT under the old key would start the version again, a store at 1 and a tenant at whatever its INSERT names, and so could bring an old witness back (for a store, one admitted at 1) (M5A-16b). A key is retired by deleting its row. Every tenant- or store-scope grant inserted, deleted (cascades included) or changed in any column but `updated_at` moves the actor's `tmpos_session.tenant_grant_version`, and so does every delete of a tenant or store such a grant names, once per actor and whatever the grant's status: as a replica no cascade takes those grants, so a row inserted again under that key would otherwise bring them back unchanged. The `t2:` witness carries both counters and 013's authority version, so an account, link or anchor changed and changed back, as a replica too, moves it as well. M5A-05b now asserts the idle store session refused, M5A-16b the re-key refused, and the race suite asserts the grant, tenant and store cases (M5T-D1–D4, M5T-H2–H5). By design a tenant-scope grant carries no store version: a store of its tenant suspended, moved in or out, or deleted leaves a tenant-scope session's witness unchanged, and that grant never satisfies a store route (M5T-G2). **Residuals, all owner writes outside every runtime role:** (a) the owner can write `tmpos_session.tenant_grant_version` (or 013's `platform_authority_version`) directly and move a counter backwards; (b) the owner of `public.tenant`, `public.store` or `public.user_membership` can disable or drop 016's triggers; (c) the owner can INSERT a tenant row at any access version, the bound included, after which 010's next suspension of it raises 22003. This revives nothing: no grant names a new tenant, and a tenant inserted again after a delete meets grant versions that delete already moved. (a) and (b) could let an old witness be met again. (d) Each actor's grant version stops at 9007199254740991: at the bound the next write that would move it fails its transaction, so a grant change or a tenant or store delete naming that actor is refused rather than left unversioned — unreachable in practice, and fail-closed. The runtime role's INSERT on `public.store` is DA-80 | M5-GIAM-D1-P2-R1 (closed in code M5-TENANT-SESSION-P1) | The first membership-management or store-lifecycle command must bring its own monotonic witness, as 010 did for tenants, and this register row is its acceptance criterion. *(M5-GIAM-D1-P3: still open. This is why the Tenant Management page exposes no store lifecycle control.)* *(M5-TENANT-SESSION-P1: closed in code; any such command now moves the versions through 016's triggers. Residuals (a)–(c) are recorded, not fixed: owner operations must not write the version tables, disable or drop 016's triggers, or insert a tenant at a chosen access version, and a replica-mode write must still repair what the skipped cascade would have done (DA-73). No store route or store permission is in production: that stays with G-IAM/D1.)* |

It holds in CI only because `test:db` runs `migrationEngine.integration.test.mjs` first, and that suite's last case begins by dropping `public.schema_migrations` (`resetTarget`), so the ledger is empty when `rlsIsolation` starts. Run against a database whose ledger already records migrations, or after the suites are reordered, it fails without any product defect | M5-GIAM-D1-P3 | Not fixed. Make S2-DB-1 establish its own starting ledger, or read the ledger as M5A-01 does, before the order of the DB suites is changed |

CI is unaffected. *(Recurred in M7A-CUSTOMERS-P3-R2: Tailwind scans the large untracked directories at the workspace root; a clean export builds fast.)* | M5-GIAM-D1-P3 | Not fixed. | DA-44 | **The tenant directory's known limits.** Display names not written through the display-name command (before migration 009 closed the runtime's direct write, or by the database owner) were never length-bounded and are not rewritten. A stored name longer than 4,096 UTF-16 code units (`TENANT_DIRECTORY_MAX_NAME_UNITS`) therefore makes any page that includes it a bounded 503 rather than an unbounded response. A tenant id outside the RFC 4122 version and variant form does the same; the column defaults to `gen_random_uuid()`, so only an out-of-band insert can hold one. Either way, the console cannot page past such a row, because the next continuation comes from the page that cannot be served; only a caller that already knows a later tenant id can build its cursor and resume. The page cannot supply the id and version to repair the row. The response's `actions` (`editDisplayName`, `changeStatus`) are presentation hints computed by the canonical rule (`createPlatformPermits`). They grant nothing, and no command may treat them as authority. No index on `public.tenant (created_at, tenant_id)` exists and migration 012 adds none, so each page reads the whole tenant table and sorts it: cost grows with the tenant count, and at a large enough count a page would exceed the 2.7 s statement bound and answer 503. The continuation is a row comparison that an index on exactly those columns would serve, so adding one changes no answer | M5-GIAM-D1-P3 | Carried to the pre-launch review. Before go-live, check the managed database for an overlong legacy name or a non-RFC tenant id, and add the `(created_at, tenant_id)` index in a later migration before the tenant count makes the page slow; the `actions` rule stays presentation-only |

| DA-46 | **The platform admission read is an oracle for the runtime role.** `tmpos_app` holds EXECUTE on `tmpos_admission.m5_admit_platform_session` and may call it with any provider reference. SQL running as `tmpos_app` (a leaked runtime credential or an injection) can therefore learn whether a UID it knows or guesses names a platform actor, and that actor's standing. The authorizer decides every request on the role's CURRENT definition, so a deploy that changes a built-in role's definition takes effect on the next request. But it moves no witness, so it ends no session: a session admitted under the old definition continues under the new one. Stated explicitly (carried unchanged through M6-RATE-P2): code-defined platform permission changes are enforced on every request; they do not currently change the durable administrative-session witness; removing a code permission and later restoring it can leave an old session usable again. | DA-48 | **The version is only as strong as its triggers.** The owner of the four tables (the principal that applies the migrations) or a superuser can disable or drop 013's triggers, or update or delete version rows, outside the migration contract. 013's section 6 is a snapshot at apply time, not a standing guarantee. A deleted version row restarts that actor at 1, where an old witness could be met again. A session running with `session_replication_role = replica` is covered only because the triggers are `ENABLE ALWAYS`: were one switched to plain `ENABLE`, replica-mode writes would move nothing. The runtime role can do none of this: it holds no privilege on the table or the trigger function and owns none of the four tables | M5-GIAM-D1-P4 | G-DBROLE: before administrative sessions carry production traffic, extend the live role-topology check with 013's trigger postcondition (exactly four triggers on the function, each `tgenabled = 'A'`), and hold every principal that owns or can become owner of the four tables to an owner's standard |
| DA-49 | **One lock per actor, and one admission read per request.** The trigger upserts one version row per actor, so two transactions that reach the same actors' version rows in opposite orders can deadlock: one actor's rows across the four tables, or grants moved between two actors in opposite directions. PostgreSQL detects it and aborts one, which commits nothing and may be retried. No routine of 001–012 writes those tables, so only provisioning and operator writes are exposed. Every protected administrative request costs one admission read. It is one read, not two: the authorizer decides on the scope that read selected (compare DA-38). A command re-checks the actor's account, platform grant, role and 007 security version inside its committing transaction (migration 009), but not 013's authority version, so a change that moves only that version after the request was admitted (the identity anchor repointed, for example) does not stop that one command; the next request is refused. *Superseded by M5-ADMIN-REVOKE-P1 ([ADR-30](./10-architecture-decision-records.md)):* a command now re-reads 013's standing inside its committing transaction and requires the witness bound to its session, and migration 015 makes an anchor repoint wait for a command holding the account, so such a change refuses the command (DA-70) or takes effect after its COMMIT | M5-GIAM-D1-P4 | Pre-launch review: write one actor's provisioning and operator changes in one fixed table order; measure the admission read with DA-33 and DA-38 when the administrative boundary carries traffic, and optimize only with the same no-window guarantee |
| DA-50 | **A tenant-plane grant flood denies platform admission.** 007 refuses an actor holding more than 200 usable grants across every scope, and 013 passes that refusal on. The administrative boundary acts on a refusal by revoking every administrative session of the principal and refusing its login. So tenant- or store-scope grants added to a platform administrator's actor can, past 200, deny its admin admission, although they move no authority version and confer no platform authority. Every direction fails closed, and this is kept; it is DA-40's blast radius, now on the administrative boundary too | M5-GIAM-D1-P4 | With DA-40: give 007's fault conditions their own outcome, mapped to an outage, when a migration next touches the resolution path |
| DA-51 | **An account profile edit moves the authority version.** 013 moves the version on any `app_user` UPDATE that changes a column other than `updated_at`, the display name included, and so ends each of that actor's administrative sessions on its next request. In ordinary operation 007's security version already moves on the same write (002's trigger refreshes `updated_at`, which 007 digests), so no session ends that would not have ended anyway: 013 only makes the witness impossible to reproduce | M5-GIAM-D1-P4 | Accepted. A later stage that wants profile edits to leave sessions alone must narrow 013's `app_user` rule and 007's digest together |
| DA-52 | **The limiter's cost on the primary database is unmeasured under real traffic.** Every protected request is at least one short transaction on the runtime kernel, which writes whenever the consume is allowed, and a login is up to three. A consume the committed state already refuses — a live window, at the clock read after that read, whose count plus the cost exceeds the limit — is answered from an unlocked read: no row lock and, once the key fingerprint is registered (the key check before it can register one), no write, no transaction ID and no WAL (measured on a disposable cluster: `pg_current_xact_id_if_assigned()` is null after it). That relies on a database clock that does not step backwards; if one does, a refusal can rarely rest on a window a concurrent consume has just reopened — a 429 where an allowance was due, never the reverse. A refusal that races the consume spending the last allowance can still take the locked path. These transactions share the kernel's ten one-connection pools with sessions, admission and commands, and consumes of one hot bucket that take the locked path serialize on its row lock, each allowance holding it through its commit's WAL flush; a consume that waits past its 200 ms lock bound is a 503. The adapter's per-bucket lanes keep one source to one connection per bucket and hold at most 64 consumes of one bucket per instance, the one in flight included (`RATE_LIMIT_LANE_DEPTH`); past that a consume of that bucket is a 503 at once. Many sources can still exhaust the ten connections, which is an edge or WAF concern. A consume aborted before its COMMIT is answered at once and releases its bucket's line when the kernel answers; the kernel cancels its statement and retires that connection, and the server rolls the abandoned transaction back only when it sees the session end. So the next consume of that bucket, from any instance, can wait on that row lock — bounded by the server-side bounds — and is a 503 if it outwaits its 200 ms lock bound. That fails closed; it is recorded, not fixed. Each window-opening allowance the adapter answers owes one reclaim (a window that opens only after its caller was answered unavailable owes none) (at most four expired rows); owed calls run one at a time per instance, the next only after one deleted a full four, and one that deletes fewer, or fails, clears what is owed. So after a burst each window opened is matched by up to four reclaimed while a backlog remains and reclaims succeed; during a burst of new clients windows can open faster than one reclaim at a time clears them, and with no traffic the backlog stays. Every window reopen is a non-HOT update (it moves the indexed `expires_at`), and reclaims leave dead entries at the low end of the expiry index. Under a long-held database snapshot (a long transaction, a replication slot, `pg_dump`) vacuum cannot clear them: with about 100,000 of them (a one-off check on a throwaway local PostgreSQL 16 cluster, not part of the committed tests) both reclaims tried hit the 50 ms statement bound and failed with SQLSTATE 57014 until the snapshot ended, and storage grew meanwhile; consumes, which reach their row by primary key, were unaffected. The only measurement is a bounded check on a local disposable cluster ("The distributed rate-limit store" below), which is not a production throughput claim | M6-RATE-P2 | Pre-launch review, with G-PERF, G-OBSERVE and DA-33, DA-38 and DA-49: measure consume latency, pool contention and bucket-table growth under real traffic; monitor reclaims failing with SQLSTATE 57014, long transactions and the xmin horizon's age, and the bucket table's bloat; put connection-level flood protection at the edge; replace the store behind the same port (ADR-26) only if that measurement justifies it |
| DA-53 | **`RATE_LIMIT_KEY` can be neither rotated nor re-registered by any procedure that exists.** Migration 014 registers the fingerprint of the FIRST key that calls and answers every other key `key_mismatch`. Nothing erases or replaces that fingerprint, by design, so an instance started with a new key refuses every protected request with a 503 and fails readiness until it holds the registered key again. Worse, a wrongly keyed instance that calls first after 014 is applied locks every correctly keyed instance out the same way (security review F3). So can a holder of the runtime database credential (any login that is a member of `tmpos_app`) without `RATE_LIMIT_KEY`: calling the consume or the probe first with a fingerprint of its choosing, between 014's apply (or a re-apply after its rollback) and the first correctly keyed call, locks every instance out (every protected request 503, readiness 503) — closed, never an allowance. The runbook step against it: right after applying 014, have a correctly keyed instance call (its readiness probe registers the key) before anything else holds that credential, or have the owner confirm that the registered fingerprint equals HMAC-SHA256 keyed with the bytes `RATE_LIMIT_KEY` decodes to (not its text) over the ASCII string `tmpos-rate-limit-key-fingerprint:v1`, as `rateLimitKeyFingerprint` computes it. The reclaim takes no key: such a holder can leave a reclaim's transaction open holding up to four expired rows, so that the next consume of those buckets waits out its lock bound and is a 503 — less than that credential allows in any case, by holding connections. Recovery from a wrong registration is an operator procedure, not a routine: stop the wrongly keyed instances, confirm the intended key's fingerprint, delete the one `tmpos_rate_limit.key_fingerprint` row as the owner in a maintenance window, and let a correctly keyed instance register. No routine does this and nothing does it automatically. No rotation procedure exists. The fingerprint also leaves the store through 014's rollback, which refuses while any bucket is inside its window and otherwise drops every bucket with it | M6-RATE-P2 | Before go-live, with G-SECRETS' rotation policy: write and rehearse the post-apply registration step and the re-registration runbook above, and design and prove a coordinated rotation procedure (a new migration if it needs one). Never a reset the runtime can reach |

PUBLIC's grants in any other schema are not read | M6-ENTRY-P1 | G-DBROLE: managed provisioning proves or accepts it |
| DA-55 | **Policy predicates are not re-read at startup.** Cross-version deparse is not stable enough to pin, so each privilege role's policy line pins its command, PERMISSIVE or RESTRICTIVE, and ROW SECURITY only; the predicates are the ones migrations 005–014 created, proven at apply by the ledger checksums. A later in-place change to a predicate is schema drift the startup does not detect | M6-ENTRY-P1 | G-DBROLE |
| DA-56 | **The preflight is a startup-time check.** It runs once, before the listener binds; a grant, membership or ledger change after that is not seen until the next start, and nothing re-reads the login's privileges or the ledger while serving. Role- and database-level defaults (`pg_db_role_setting`, e.g. `session_replication_role`) are not read at startup either: a default set there applies to every runtime session unseen | M6-ENTRY-P1 | G-DBROLE: managed provisioning must forbid live grant changes on the runtime login, or restart after any, and must prove that the login and its database carry no such default |

Nothing claims "no spend" | M6-ENTRY-P1 | Accepted; rotation and re-registration stay DA-53's |

A session-store outage after the entry starts is therefore seen only by protected traffic, which fails closed with the bounded 503 — never a credential verdict — while readiness still reports ready | M6-ENTRY-P1 (recorded M5-ADMIN-STEPUP-P1) | Targeted pre-deployment follow-up: add a bounded session-store probe to readiness, or record why the load balancer need not see it. Not redesigned here |
| DA-60 | **An unexplained rate-limiter test sensitivity.** In M6-ENTRY-P1 a local replay of the rate-limiter suites in CI order occasionally failed once with `lock_timeout` (SQLSTATE 55P03) under host load, on the first commit's candidate only, never on the base commit (2/2 local replays, and 3/3 in M6-RATE-P2). The exact-commit CI runs were green on both commits. Attributing it to a pre-existing load sensitivity rests on code-path reasoning only; no run isolated a cause. *M5-TENANT-SESSION-P1 characterised a related failure of M6R-PERF and M6R-13 (`tests/db/rateLimiter.integration.test.mjs`) and M6RS-03 (`tests/db/rateLimitRuntime.integration.test.mjs`) in local CI-order replays: consumes answered unavailable (503) or were limited when a burst ran past the limiter's own deadline, `LIMITER_DEADLINE_MS` = 500 ms (`server/runtime/rateLimit.ts`), under host CPU contention (bursts of 506–702 ms). Standalone runs of `rateLimiter` with and without added busy loops failed once in seven on the base commit (`a4328974`) and once in seven on the candidate, so the sensitivity predates this stage. A green rerun is not a repair; it stays open.* *(M7B-INVOICES-P1: a local CI-order replay of the stage's server candidate failed once at its thirteenth step, the rate-limiter suites, with M6R-03, M6R-13 and M6R-PERF red under host load, and that step passed 15 of 15 alone; the next full replay passed every step. Same class; nothing changed.)* *(M6R-PERF-R1, 2026-10-10: the M6R-03 failures are explained and the test repaired — a consume deep in a line reaching its 500 ms deadline is the contract's fail-closed answer, and M6R-03 now proves exactness consume by consume; the adapter defect that widened it is corrected (a waiter past its deadline sent a transaction aborted at once), and a latent one found in review (a late answer obeyed), each pinned by a regression. Still open here: M6R-PERF's "nothing unavailable" and M6RS-07, probably the same family but not reproduced, and the original local `55P03` replay — consistent with a stalled client holding the row, not isolated.)* *(M6R-PERF-R2, 2026-10-10: M6R-PERF's three "nothing unavailable" CI failures and M6RS-07's one were assessed separately; M6R-PERF's were placed in two fail-closed classes, each reproduced locally with a schedule the test owns, and M6RS-07's, probably, in the second (wording qualified in M6R-PERF-R3). A consume reaching the 500 ms deadline while waiting in a hot bucket's line: all 4 unavailable answers in 36568875233 and 27 of the 29 in 36883413179. A consume reaching the 200 ms row-lock bound (`55P03`): all 3 in 37453777754, the other 2 in 36883413179, and — by the one lock timeout CI's PostgreSQL log shows in M6RS-07's failing wave (36996687809), whose odd reply was never printed — probably M6RS-07's. Holding a COMMIT back reproduced 37453777754's signature and M6RS-07's exact assertion and counts — a mechanism consistent with M6RS-07's failure, not proof of what caused it; CI's logs establish the lock timeouts, not what held the rows. No runtime defect was demonstrated, and what delayed the holders in CI is not attributed. The original local `55P03` replay is consistent with the second mechanism but still not isolated. DA-60 stays OPEN: availability under load is G-PERF's and an owner decision.)* *(M6R-PERF-R3, 2026-10-11: evidence tightened, nothing explained further — the adapter's exact deadline is now proved against the adapter itself, transaction outcomes are named by the kernel's phases, and the owner-side reconciliation applies per bucket only where the snapshot shows the run's one window (or no row before any window of the run could have ended). DA-60 stays OPEN.)* *(M7B-INVOICES-ISSUE-P1, [ADR-50](./10-architecture-decision-records.md): a new serialization point. Issues of one store serialize on its number counter row from the lock to COMMIT, and a settings write can wait behind issues holding the settings row FOR SHARE ([12](./12-m7b-invoice-issue-decision-pack.md) §10.7). Its latency under load is unmeasured; DA-60 is otherwise unchanged and G-PERF stays OPEN.)* | M6-ENTRY-P1 (recorded M5-ADMIN-STEPUP-P1; characterised M5-TENANT-SESSION-P1) | Targeted pre-deployment follow-up: reproduce under controlled load and explain it. The limiter's thresholds and timeouts are never loosened to make it pass |
| DA-61 | **Step-up needs the prior session to be still valid.** The reauthentication mode renews only a session that still authenticates; that is what ties the new sign-in to the administrator who started the action. If that session expired, idled out or was revoked while the dialog was open, the reauthentication is refused, the console ends the session, and the administrator signs in fully; the pending action is not resumed, and a new one needs a new reason and key. Two further behaviours are those of an ordinary login. **(a) A store failure during rotation** *(rewritten M6-ADMIN-AUDIT-P1)*. On the administrative boundary, ending the presented session, creating the new one and writing the audit record are one transaction (`establish`). A failure before its commit answers the bounded 503 with no cookie and leaves the presented session exactly as it was. A deadline or lost connection during COMMIT is an outcome unknown: the answer is the same 503, but the presented session may have been ended and the new session and its record may exist, never delivered. The browser then holds no usable session, and the administrator signs in again. The tenant boundary is unchanged: revoke, then create, so a create that fails after the revoke answers 503 and leaves no session. *(Since M5-TENANT-SESSION-P1 the tenant boundary uses `establish` too, so the same holds there.)* **(b) Two concurrent reauthentications presenting one cookie** *(rewritten M5-ADMIN-REVOKE-P1)*. On the administrative boundary at most one succeeds. Each `establish` locks the actor's account first (migration 009, through its standing check), so the two serialize. A reauthentication must end the presented session in its own transaction, so the second finds it already ended and answers 401 `login_reauth_no_session`, creating, ending and recording nothing. One successor session exists, and one record names the presented session as `replacedSessionRef`. The tenant boundary, where no client or entry uses the mode, still revokes and then creates, so two reauthentications there can both succeed. *(Closed for the tenant boundary by M5-TENANT-SESSION-P1: a tenant recent sign-in must end the tenant session it presents in its own `establish`, so at most one of several concurrent ones succeeds (M5T-F3), and one whose session a logout ended first is 401 `login_reauth_no_session` (M5T-F1, M5T-K3).)* | M5-ADMIN-STEPUP-P1 (a) rewritten M6-ADMIN-AUDIT-P1 (b) rewritten M5-ADMIN-REVOKE-P1; tenant (a) and (b) M5-TENANT-SESSION-P1 | Accepted by design. (a) is narrowed for the administrative boundary by the atomic `establish`, and its outcome-unknown case is accepted. (b) is closed for the administrative boundary by M5-ADMIN-REVOKE-P1; on the tenant boundary it is accepted and not changed. *(M5-TENANT-SESSION-P1: (a) is narrowed and (b) closed for the tenant boundary too.)* |
| DA-62 | **Recency rests on the provider's `auth_time`.** The server takes the authentication time only from the verified token's `auth_time`, which a token refresh does not move, and the admin login policy refuses one older than 5 minutes (`login_auth_stale`), so a refreshed token cannot open or renew a session. The in-page reauthentication is the provider's own sign-in (password or Google, then TOTP); the server never sees a provider "reauthenticate" call, only the resulting token, so a provider that reissued `auth_time` without a real sign-in would defeat the rule. Google popup sign-in under the admin CSP is still unexercised against the live provider (DA-8) | M5-ADMIN-STEPUP-P1 | Pre-launch review, with DA-8's live-provider check: confirm a fresh sign-in moves `auth_time` and a refresh does not |
| DA-63 | **A suspension racing live tenant requests can be answered 403** *(resolved M5-ADMIN-REVOKE-P1)*. Subtest M5A-09 of `tests/db/tenantAdmission.integration.test.mjs` used to accept only 200 or 401 from four requests in flight while the tenant is suspended, and one answer was sometimes 403. Interleaved replays reproduced it at `a2cc223` (1 of 4) and on `7813bd1` (2 of 7), and it failed M5-ADMIN-STEPUP-P1's CI attempt 2. **It is a correct, fail-closed refusal.** The boundary admitted the request, the suspension then committed, and the route authorizer's own second read (DA-38) answered 403 `authz_denied` on what the database said by then. The refused request changed nothing, and the next request is 401. The stage's reproduction at `1e14207` observed exactly that. **The test now permits only 200, 401 and 403, and asserts the safety properties of each outcome** (M5A-09, line 805): every answer is 200, 401 or 403; a 200 carries the full scope and a refusal only the fixed body; each 403 is `authz_denied`, and there are never more 403s than recorded authorizer reads that refused; each 401 is `session_admission_denied` or `session_unknown`, never an `_unrevoked` reason; the tenant row moved exactly once; no audit record or outbox event was written; every stamp written follows a served answer; the next request is 401 with no session left; and after reactivation the old cookie is still 401. **M5A-09b** (line 908) forces the 403 interleaving: it holds the authorizer's read until the suspension commits, and shows the session row unchanged and the store asked to write nothing | M5-ADMIN-STEPUP-P1 (recorded M6-ADMIN-AUDIT-P1; resolved M5-ADMIN-REVOKE-P1) | Resolved as a classification: a legitimate fail-closed 403. A 403 is accepted only as `authz_denied` and only when a refused authorizer read accounts for it; any other status still fails the test. The stage's M5A-09 and M5A-09b runs predate the final runtime and the final 015; the stage's final verification and CI re-run them. The tenant plane's second read is DA-38's design, outside ADR-30's scope |
| DA-64 | **Logout can end a session without its record.** Administrative logout puts revocation first. When the audited `end` fails (the record refused, an outage, a timeout, an answer that is neither word), the plain revocation runs, and on success the answer is 204. The record is then absent, or present if the first commit's answer was lost. The request log says `logout_audit_unconfirmed`, and a warn-level `session_logout_audit_unconfirmed` event is emitted before the fallback runs, so a defect that turned every logout into the fallback is visible. The event is emitted even when the fallback also fails and the answer is 503 with the cookie cleared. This is non-atomic by choice: a logout must not leave the session live because its record could not be written. *Since M5-TENANT-SESSION-P1 the tenant logout works the same way (M5T-J4): the warn event and the log reason are the same, and when the fallback fails too the cookie is still cleared and the answer is 503, but the tenant session stays live until its idle expiry (30 minutes after its last use) or its absolute expiry (8 hours), as an administrative one does until 15 minutes or 1 hour.* | M6-ADMIN-AUDIT-P1 | Accepted by design. Operations alert on the warn event before production traffic. G-AUDIT stays OPEN |
| DA-65 | **Session changes that are not audited.** Only a successful administrative login, reauthentication and logout write a row. *(Delivered for the tenant audience by M5-TENANT-SESSION-P1: the tenant boundary's successful login, recent sign-in and logout now write `tenant.session.*` rows, so "every tenant-boundary session change" below narrows to the same classes as on the administrative boundary.)* Unaudited: the revocation of every session of a principal after an admission denial at login or at revalidation; the revocation of the presented session on a changed witness; idle and absolute expiry, including the expired row a logout deletes when it finds nothing live (`end` answering `absent`); every tenant-boundary session change; refused and unverified attempts (bad token, bad or missing TOTP, stale `auth_time`, rate limit, reauthentication refusals); and provider outages | M6-ADMIN-AUDIT-P1 | G-AUDIT: decide which of these classes must be audited, and how (a refused attempt has no trusted actor), before production traffic |
| DA-66 | **The database does not couple a session to its record.** The pairing is the application's. The PostgreSQL adapter's `create` refuses an administrative record, so `establish` is the only application path to one. But the runtime login still holds EXECUTE on `m5_session_create` and `m5_session_revoke` and could call them directly, creating or ending an administrative session with no row. Closing that needs a routine migration (an audited definer routine, or per-audience routines), which this stage does not add. *Since M5-TENANT-SESSION-P1 this covers tenant sessions too: the adapter's `create` still writes a tenant record with no row (no boundary calls it any more), and the runtime login may call the routines directly for either audience.* *M7A-CUSTOMERS-P2: migration 018 revokes `tmpos_app`'s EXECUTE on 011's `m5_session_create`, so every create goes through `tmpos_mint.m7a_session_create`. The administrative branch stays unproven: it takes no proof and is 011's create unchanged, so the role can still create an administrative session with no audit row (DA-95, the class of DA-37). `m5_session_revoke` stays directly callable for either audience.* | M6-ADMIN-AUDIT-P1 | G-DBROLE / G-AUDIT: decide with DA-37's per-audience routines |
| DA-67 | **A possible deadlock between `establish` and a principal-wide revocation.** `establish` locks the presented session's row (the touch), and then `m5_session_create` deletes that principal's sessions that have been expired for more than a day. `m5_session_revoke_principal` deletes all of the principal's sessions in scan order, so the two can lock the same rows in opposite orders. PostgreSQL would then abort one of them after `deadlock_timeout`: the login answers 503, or the denial reads as `login_admission_denied_unrevoked`. This follows from the lock order and has not been reproduced. *Unchanged by M5-ADMIN-REVOKE-P1:* `establish` now locks the actor's account, links and grants first, but it still touches the presented session before `m5_session_create` prunes, and a principal-wide revocation takes no account lock, so the two can still meet on session rows in opposite orders. *M5-TENANT-SESSION-P1: the tenant `establish` takes the same order after its hold (016's hold, then the presented session, then the prune), so the same applies to tenant sessions; not reproduced.* | M6-ADMIN-AUDIT-P1 | Targeted pre-deployment follow-up: reproduce, or order the locks. Fails closed either way |
| DA-68 | **The audited pair shares one deadline.** `establish` and `end` run up to five statements (get, touch, revoke, create, insert) in one transaction under one port deadline (3,000 ms) and the store bounds (lock 2,100 ms, statement 2,700 ms). Before this stage the rotation was two calls with a deadline each. Under contention an operation now ends at the caller's deadline and fails closed with a 503, and a deadline reached during COMMIT is an outcome unknown more often (DA-61(a)). *M5-TENANT-SESSION-P1: the tenant `establish` runs 016's hold and the v2 re-read first, so up to seven statements (hold, re-read, get, touch, revoke, create, insert) share that deadline, and a login waiting on a held row spends it (DA-78).* | M6-ADMIN-AUDIT-P1 | Measure under load before production traffic. The bounds are not widened to hide contention |
| DA-69 | **The kernel's result objects keep the connection's cancel key.** `supervisedPgClient.ts` calls `Reflect.deleteProperty(rows, 'state')` so that no result carries the backend's `{ pid, secret }`. Today no result object leaves an adapter: the session store reads only `.count` and `.command` of its insert result. This predates this stage and was found during its review | M6-ADMIN-AUDIT-P1 (pre-existing) | Targeted follow-up: copy rows out instead of deleting, or record why the key is harmless in process. | DA-70 | **A command refused inside its transaction reads as an outage.** When a logout, a revocation or a change to the actor's standing commits before a tenant-management command takes its locks, the command commits nothing: no mutation, idempotency completion, audit record or event. It answers 503 (body `service_unavailable`, reason `transaction_unavailable`). This is the store's existing non-disclosure rule: a refusal inside the committing transaction gives the answer an outage gives, so nothing distinguishes a revoked actor from a database that could not answer (`postgresTransactionalStore.ts`, the revalidation step). The console therefore sees an unavailable service, not a revocation. The next request is the definitive answer: 401 `session_unknown` after a logout, `session_admission_denied` after a grant removal, `session_version_mismatch` after a removal and restoration (`tests/db/adminRevocation.integration.test.mjs` M5R-03a, M5R-04a, M5R-04c) | M5-ADMIN-REVOKE-P1 | Accepted by design. Not changed |
| DA-71 | **A logout racing a recent sign-in can end nothing.** If the recent sign-in commits first, it has already ended the presented session and issued its successor. A logout that presented the old cookie then finds nothing live: it answers 204 (`logout_session_absent`) and writes no record, and the successor stays live until its own logout, idle timeout or absolute expiry (M5R-07b). In the other order the logout ends the session, and the recent sign-in is refused with 401 `login_reauth_no_session`, creating and recording nothing (M5R-07a). Both orders follow the ordering rule: whichever commits first takes effect. The stage's reproduction at `1e14207` observed the first order too. *The tenant boundary behaves the same since M5-TENANT-SESSION-P1 (M5T-F1, M5T-F2, M5T-K3, M5T-K4).* | M5-ADMIN-REVOKE-P1 | Recorded, not changed |
| DA-72 | **Migration 015 makes operator writes wait, and relies on the account table's row-security posture.** *Waits.* An anchor repoint, and an identity link inserted for an actor or re-pointed to it, now wait for any in-flight command or login that holds that actor's account, up to that transaction's own deadline. A grant (any scope) inserted or re-pointed and an anchor delete already waited in an ordinary session through their foreign keys, and now wait in replica mode too (DA-73). That includes provisioning and grant administration. A profile-only update (email, display name), the identity sync's upsert and a link re-pointed away from the held actor do not wait on the account, because 015 locks the new actor only (`tests/db/platformAdmission.integration.test.mjs` M5P-10, with the account held alone). A link moved away from an actor whose command or login holds 009's locks still waits on its own row. A changer transaction that already holds another row of the same actor that 009 locks (written earlier in the same transaction) can still meet a command in the opposite order; PostgreSQL aborts one side, which commits nothing. Applying or rolling back 015 holds a SHARE ROW EXCLUSIVE lock or stronger on `platform_identity`, `identity_link` and `user_membership` until its transaction ends, so writes to them wait. *Row security.* The lock is taken by the trigger function's owner (`SECURITY DEFINER`) and can lock only rows that owner sees. `public.app_user` has row security enabled with no policy, so the lock relies, as 009's definer routine does, on that owner acting as the table's owner and on FORCE ROW LEVEL SECURITY being off. Forced, the owner would see no row and the lock would silently serialize nothing. 015's postcondition refuses to finish in that state (M5P-11), but it is an apply-time snapshot: a later FORCE, or a trigger disabled or dropped by the owner (as for 013's, DA-48), is not re-read at startup. The preflight pins 015's checksum only | M5-ADMIN-REVOKE-P1 | Operations: never force row security on `public.app_user` or disable 015's triggers; the live role-topology check (G-DBROLE) should read both. Measure changer waits under load with DA-68 |
| DA-73 | **Replica mode.** A session with `session_replication_role = replica` runs no foreign-key check and no cascade. Before 015 such a writer waited on nothing: a grant inserted for or re-pointed to an actor, and an anchor delete, could pass a command or login that held the account. 015's triggers are `ENABLE ALWAYS`, as 013's are, so those writes wait in replica mode too (M5P-09 runs every case in both modes). 015 does not restore the checks and cascades themselves: a replica-mode anchor delete leaves the account behind (M5P-09 asserts it) | M5-ADMIN-REVOKE-P1 | Recorded. A replica-mode write to these tables is an owner operation outside the runtime and must repair what the skipped cascades would have done |
| DA-74 | **Two operator writes can deadlock each other.** In both cases PostgreSQL detects the cycle and aborts one writer, which commits nothing, so there is no stale commit. **(a) Opposite re-points (pre-existing).** A link or platform-scope grant re-pointed from actor A to B, racing one re-pointed from B to A (tenant- and store-scope grants move no version row): 013's trigger upserts the old actor's version row first and the new actor's second (013's trigger function, `v_before` then `v_after`), so the two meet in opposite orders on those rows. This is DA-49's case. 015 adds no cycle to it: its FOR KEY SHARE locks on the accounts do not conflict with each other. **(b) An anchor delete racing an anchor key move (new with 015).** An anchor DELETE of actor A holds A's anchor row and takes FOR KEY SHARE on A's account (015). A concurrent primary-key move of another anchor onto A's id also takes FOR KEY SHARE on A's account, then waits on the uniqueness check behind the uncommitted delete. The delete's cascade then needs A's account row exclusively and waits on the mover. Moving an identity's primary key is something no application path does, and the window is only between the delete's trigger and its end-of-statement cascade. Derived from the lock order; not reproduced | M5-ADMIN-REVOKE-P1 ((a) pre-existing, 013) | Recorded, not fixed. Provisioning and operator changes write one actor's rows in one fixed order (DA-49) and never move an identity's primary key |
| DA-75 | **A tenant delete can deadlock with a command on that tenant (pre-existing).** A command holds every grant of its actor FOR SHARE (009, step 3) before its mutator locks the target tenant row FOR UPDATE (009's display-name routine; 010's status routine). An operator's tenant DELETE locks the tenant row first, and its cascade then deletes the grants at that tenant (002: `user_membership.tenant_id ... on delete cascade`, and through its stores `store_id`). When the command's actor holds a grant at the tenant being deleted, the two take those rows in opposite orders, and PostgreSQL aborts one: the command answers 503 and commits nothing, or the delete fails. A store DELETE alone only waits, because no command locks a store row. 015 places no trigger on a grant DELETE and does not change this. Found by the stage's design review from the lock order; not reproduced | M5-ADMIN-REVOKE-P1 (pre-existing) | Targeted follow-up before any tenant-delete operation exists: order the locks, or refuse the delete while a command can hold them. Fails closed either way |
| DA-76 | **The 200-grant bound depends on tenant and store status, which nothing locks.** 007 refuses an actor holding more than 200 usable grants across every scope, and a tenant or store grant is usable only while its tenant (and store) is neither suspended nor pending activation (007's resolution); 013 passes that refusal on (DA-50). 009 and 015 lock the actor's account, links and grants, never a tenant or store row, so a status change that moves the actor across the bound is not ordered against a login or a command. A reactivation that pushes the actor over the bound after their standing read does not stop that login or command. | DA-77 | **Stamp updates are conditional on liveness only (administrative boundary).** The pre-operation security requirement (G-CPLOGIN) asks the durable store to make `validatedAt` and `lastSeenAt` updates conditional on the record still being live AND on the principal's current security version. The second half is not implemented: `update` rewrites the stamp by key through `m5_session_touch`, which answers `absent` for an ended session and checks no version (`postgresSessionStore.ts`, `update`). | DA-78 | **016's hold makes standing writes wait, and a writer in the opposite order can deadlock with a tenant login.** *Waits.* While a tenant login's `establish` holds its actor's standing, these wait for its COMMIT, up to that transaction's own deadline: an account write; a change or removal of the actor's links or grants; a grant or link inserted for the actor or re-pointed to it; an anchor change; and an UPDATE or DELETE of any tenant or store the actor's grants name. Logins of different actors that overlap in time share their FOR SHARE lock on that tenant row, so while the command waits further logins can still take it, and a run of overlapping logins can hold the command off until its own transaction's lock bound (a 503 that commits nothing). `tests/db/tenantAdmission.integration.test.mjs` M5A-19 shows six of these waiting (010's command, a store status write, a grant inserted for the actor, a grant disabled, a link disabled, an account write), and the race suite shows a suspension, a grant removal, a role change, an account-status change and an anchor repoint waiting for a login (M5T-C2, M5T-E2a–E2d). *Deadlock.* A writer that, in one transaction, updates a tenant or store row and then a grant of the same actor (or makes any multi-row write in the opposite order to the hold's) can meet a login that holds that grant and waits for that tenant or store row. PostgreSQL aborts one side, which commits nothing: the login answers 503, or the writer's change is rolled back while the login's standing was current. Neither commits a session or a change under stale authority. A normal-mode tenant or store delete whose cascade takes an actor's grants in its own order is such a write. So are two concurrent grant writes that move tenant or store grants between the same two actors in opposite directions: `m5_tenant_grant_moved` moves the old actor's grant version, then the new one's, so each can wait for the other's version row. No routine of 001–016 writes `public.user_membership`. Among the writes of 009, 010, 013 and 015 no other pair was found that takes these rows in the opposite order (009's command takes the account first, as the hold does). Derived from the lock order; not reproduced | M5-TENANT-SESSION-P1 | Recorded, not fixed (by design). | DA-95 | **Administrative minting stays unproven.** 018's administrative branch takes no proof and is 011's create unchanged, so `tmpos_app` can still create an administrative session row with a key and an `authenticated_at` of its choosing, presentable on the admin host (the class of DA-37). | DA-96 | **A stolen session store key plus the runtime role reads that session's tenant.** It reads until database-clock expiry, a revocation or a standing change, and it bypasses the idle timeout (the key holder can move `last_seen_at` through `m5_session_touch`), `view_customers` (decided in the runtime) and the host guards. A transaction whose snapshot predates a revocation or a key change keeps reading what that snapshot shows until expiry. The key is never returned by a routine or selectable by `tmpos_app`; it reaches the database only as a bound parameter | M7A-CUSTOMERS-P2 | G-DBROLE: bound old snapshots with `idle_in_transaction_session_timeout` or `transaction_timeout` on the login; parameter logging off (DA-102) |
| DA-97 | **The runtime role's other levers remain.** 007's resolver, the admission reads and 005's self-set row-security context still disclose identity and membership data to `tmpos_app`, and still drive 012's directory (DA-45) and the 009/010 commands. The self-set context also lets `tmpos_app` alone read any tenant's tenant, store and entitlement rows, which are tenant business data, and still allows the store INSERT and the two column UPDATEs (DA-29). 018 removes only two of the role-alone paths, the customer read and the session create. *(M7A-CUSTOMERS-P3, [ADR-34](./10-architecture-decision-records.md): 019 adds a lever for a holder of `tmpos_app` plus a live minted session key, for example a stolen key or a compromised tenant process. The role alone, without a key, creates nothing: 019 adds no role-alone lever and removes none.)* *(M7A-CUSTOMERS-P3-R1, [ADR-35](./10-architecture-decision-records.md): 020 adds two more. `tmpos_role.m7a_role_levels` answers any tenant's stored Customers levels to `tmpos_app` with no session key (DA-121), and `tmpos_role.m7a_role_level_set` changes a tenant's levels for a holder of `tmpos_app` plus a live store-owner key, with no audit record (DA-119). 019's create lever moves to 020's create and is judged on the presenting session's role's effective Customers level, an owner's stored edit included (DA-118). It fails closed. `tests/db/tenantSessionMint.integration.test.mjs` M7M-12 pins the rendering and checks that no caller setting moves it | M7A-CUSTOMERS-P2 | Run M7M-12 again before any PostgreSQL major-version change |
