| DA-100 | **Rolling back 018.** Its rollback restores `tmpos_app`'s EXECUTE on 011's create and 017's page, which re-opens the DA-83 exposure by design, and runtime code built for 018 then cannot create a session of either audience (503 at every login): code and database roll back together. It refuses unless it runs under READ COMMITTED, and while the issuer key or the ledger holds a row, so the owner deletes both deliberately first and installs a fresh key on re-apply. 017's rollback does not know about 018. Run beneath it (after the customer table is emptied, which it requires), it leaves `tmpos_mint` in place, and `m7a_customer_page_v2` raises `3F000` (schema `tmpos_customer` does not exist) for a live minted session (the route answers any database failure with its bounded 503: reasoned, not tested for this case); a key that fails an earlier check still answers `denied`. Re-applying 017 over 018 re-grants `tmpos_app` EXECUTE on the old page, and the startup preflight then refuses the login with `role_privilege_unexpected` alone. Both are tested in a rolled-back transaction (M7M-09b). After 018's own rollback the preflight answers `role_privilege_missing` and `role_privilege_unexpected` (M7M-09, M7M-02b). The chain rolls back newest first | M7A-CUSTOMERS-P2 | G-MIGRATE: the rollback runbook |
| DA-101 | **Installing the issuer key is an owner step outside the migrations.** It is a new operational dependency. The database owner installs `TENANT_SESSION_ISSUER_KEY`'s bytes by a bound parameter from the secrets store, in a session whose statement and parameter logging is off (ADR-33), never as a literal, in a migration, in a repository file or through a dashboard. The key must be at least 32 bytes from a CSPRNG, never derived from a passphrase: the mint and the key check are unthrottled verifiers of a guessed key. The tenant entry's key parser checks length and refuses the recorded test-vector key and any key of fewer than 16 distinct byte values (a fresh 32-byte CSPRNG key has about 30, so a false refusal is negligible), which is a floor, not an entropy check. The issuer key must also differ from the administrative process's `IDEMPOTENCY_KEY`; each entry refuses to start while the OTHER's named variable is present (the administrative entry refuses `TENANT_SESSION_ISSUER_KEY`, `tenant_issuer_key_present`; the tenant entry refuses `IDEMPOTENCY_KEY`, `idempotency_key_present`), which enforces only that neither process holds the other's variable under its own name. That neither process holds issuer-equivalent bytes under another name is not code-enforced, so nothing compares them, and that is an owner custody obligation. Without the key every otherwise valid tenant mint is `refused` and every read of a well-formed key `denied`. *(M7A-CUSTOMERS-P3, [ADR-34](./10-architecture-decision-records.md): the tenant entry no longer refuses `IDEMPOTENCY_KEY`. It now requires its own, and refuses one equal to its issuer key or to `RATE_LIMIT_KEY` (`idempotency_key_shared`), so `idempotency_key_present` is gone. The administrative entry still refuses `TENANT_SESSION_ISSUER_KEY`. The tenant process's key is therefore compared with its issuer key, but the administrative process's `IDEMPOTENCY_KEY` is still compared with nothing: that it holds no issuer-equivalent bytes stays an owner custody obligation.)* | M7A-CUSTOMERS-P2 | G-DBROLE and G-MIGRATE prerequisite. Delivered in code by the tenant entry (commit 2): `tenant_issuer_key_weak` (the recorded test-vector key as HMAC sees it, so its zero-padded form too, or fewer than 16 distinct byte values), beside `_missing`, `_invalid` and `_shared` (the same HMAC key as `RATE_LIMIT_KEY`). The owner's install, the key's CSPRNG provenance, its custody and its difference from `IDEMPOTENCY_KEY` stay open (owner) |
| DA-102 | **Database logging must not capture bind parameters.** A logged session key is a read with the runtime role (DA-96). *(Since M7A-CUSTOMERS-P3 it is also a customer create with the runtime role (DA-97), and a customer's name is a bind parameter of 019's routine, so a logged parameter also exposes personal data.)* *(Since M7A-CUSTOMERS-P3-R1 a logged store-owner key is also an unaudited role-level change, DA-119.)* *(Since M7A-CUSTOMERS-P8 a search text is a bind parameter of 025's routine, so a logged parameter also exposes what a user searched for.)* A logged mint proof whose login rolled back stays usable until its expiry and creates a session that would not otherwise exist; one read before its mint commits can be raced, and the real login then answers `conflict` (503). Enforced by the tenant entry at startup (`database_logging_unsafe`) and as a readiness check (commit 2; `evaluateParameterLogging`, `server/persistence/postgresStartupPreflight.ts`), naming each setting at fault: it refuses unless `log_parameter_max_length_on_error = 0`; either `log_parameter_max_length = 0`, or `log_statement` is `none` or `ddl` with `log_min_duration_statement` and `log_min_duration_sample` at -1 and `log_transaction_sample_rate` at 0; `debug_print_plan` is `off`; auto_explain, when loaded, has `auto_explain.log_min_duration` at `-1` (`auto_explain_parameters`); and pgaudit, when loaded, has `pgaudit.log_parameter` `off` (`pgaudit_parameters`), whatever `pgaudit.log` says, since object auditing through `pgaudit.role` logs statements, including ones inside routines, with their parameters, independently of `pgaudit.log`. An absent module passes; a core setting it cannot read refuses (`log_setting_unreadable`). The guard covers the setting-driven logging paths the login can read. It does not cover error-DETAIL paths, it cannot read `shared_preload_libraries`, and it re-reads at readiness, not per statement (DA-105) | M7A-CUSTOMERS-P2 | G-DBROLE prerequisite. Checked in code at tenant startup and readiness (commit 2); logging provisioned so that nothing is captured stays an owner check, with `shared_preload_libraries` and error-DETAIL paths (DA-105) |
| DA-103 | **`m5_session_revoke_principal` has no caller binding (pre-existing).** `tmpos_app` may end every session of any principal whose key it can compute, a denial of service, and the count it answers tells whether that principal held sessions. 018 does not change it | Migration 011; recorded M7A-CUSTOMERS-P2 | Carried under G-DBROLE |
| DA-104 | **Clock skew and key rotation fail closed, as outages.** The two 018 routines judge time on the database clock (ADR-33). A runtime clock more than 60 s ahead of the database's makes every tenant mint `invalid` (503 at login), and a database clock ahead of the runtime's refuses a read up to the skew before expiry. Replacing the key is a hard cut: until a tenant process runs with the new key its logins answer 503, there is no overlap window, and every session minted under the old key stops reading | M7A-CUSTOMERS-P2 | Accepted; clock synchronization is an operations item (G-OBSERVE) |
| DA-105 | **The logging judgment is re-read by readiness, not per statement.** The tenant entry's guard (DA-102) runs at startup and then as a readiness check shared per `PROBE_REUSE_MS`, over one pooled connection. An `ALTER SYSTEM` and a reload change live sessions (observed on disposable PostgreSQL), so after one, bind parameters can be logged until the next readiness probe answers 503 and the orchestrator acts on it; the guard cannot un-log what was logged meanwhile. An `ALTER ROLE … SET` reaches only sessions opened after it, so a connection opened earlier shows the old value until the pool reconnects. auto_explain's settings are readable by the login once it is loaded (observed); pgaudit's are inferred to be (not installed on the test cluster). The guard covers the setting-driven paths it reads, not error-DETAIL paths and not `shared_preload_libraries`, which the login cannot read (42501) | M7A-CUSTOMERS-P2 | Owner and operations (G-DBROLE): logging provisioned so that no parameter is ever captured; the guard is a tripwire, not the control |
| DA-106 | **Readiness does not ask the identity provider or the audit writer.** Neither entry's `/readiness` probes the identity provider's token verification or the audit writer the tenant login and logout write through; a failure there is seen only by the requests that need it, not by readiness. This is the existing contract, the same on the administrative entry (ADR-27), and is recorded, not changed. The logging check reads one pooled connection (DA-105). *(M7A-CUSTOMERS-P3, [ADR-34](./10-architecture-decision-records.md): the tenant customer create now also writes through the audit writer. The tenant entry's readiness adds the idempotency and command-transaction probes (006's `m6_store_probe`), which prove the store's probe routine, not the audit INSERT. No probe calls 019: its EXECUTE is proven at startup only.)* | M7A-CUSTOMERS-P2 | G-OBSERVE |
| DA-107 | **The edge must route the tenant hostname to the tenant entry alone and pass Host unaltered.** The tenant entry serves every route but the probes only when Host names `SESSION_TENANT_ORIGINS`'s one authority; it never reads X-Forwarded-Host, and a Host carrying `:443` is refused with the bounded 404. This is DA-9's analogue for the tenant host and is not verified here: no real hostname or edge exists. Both processes must hold the same `RATE_LIMIT_KEY`, since 014 keeps one fingerprint per database. The tenant entry's refusal of a tenant host that the administrative origin names (`session_tenant_origin_shares_admin_host`, and the session composition's own check) compares against the tenant process's own copy of `SESSION_ADMIN_ORIGINS`: it is a consistency check, not a proof about the deployed administrative host, so the owner supplies the identical value to both processes. *(M7A-CUSTOMERS-P3-R2, [ADR-36](./10-architecture-decision-records.md): the shop build and `/api/v1/*` must also be served on ONE origin, the tenant host, with Host preserved, because the shop calls relative same-origin paths. | DA-108 | **The customer readiness probe stops at the routine's unknown-key answer.** `probeCustomerDirectory` asks `m7a_customer_page_v2` (since M7A-CUSTOMERS-P3-R1, 020's `m7a_customer_page_v3`, which answers an unknown key exactly as page_v2 does, before any later read) for a random key no session holds and requires its one `denied` row, which proves the login's EXECUTE and the routine's own reads of the session and its ledger row. 018 answers an unknown key before it reads the issuer key, 016's admission or 017's page; the first two are readiness checks of their own, and 017's page is pinned only by the startup ledger's checksum. An owner-side breakage of 017's page alone therefore shows as the route's bounded 503s, not as readiness. A full-path probe needs a minted probe session (a write) or a readiness routine in a migration; neither was in that stage (no 019), and 019 and 020 add none. *Amended (commit-2 review, CX-C1/R-E):* a second, non-mutating probe, `probeSessionMint`, calls `tmpos_mint.m7a_session_create` with every argument NULL and requires exactly `invalid`, which 018 answers before any read or write; startup step 10 and readiness add it as `session_mint`. It proves the login's EXECUTE on the mint routine and the routine's presence — not its proof, standing or insert paths, the same shape of gap this entry already records for the customer probe. *(M7A-CUSTOMERS-P8: `probeCustomerSearch` asks 025's routine the same way, last in the reads probe, with the same shape of gap: a random key's `denied` proves the login's EXECUTE and the routine's session read, not its match or its page.)* | M7A-CUSTOMERS-P2 | Accepted for this stage; G-OBSERVE |
| DA-109 | **Database tests could fall back to an ambient database; closed in the test harness, with two residuals.** In M7A-CUSTOMERS-P2 a CI-order replay gave `npm run test:db` a socket-form `TM_POS_TEST_DATABASE_URL`. The suites stripped its `?host=` and re-supplied the socket only for a cluster they had started themselves, so postgres.js filled host, port, user and password from the ambient `PG*` settings and authenticated to a managed server (it stopped at database selection; no SQL ran). M7A-DBTEST-ISOLATION-P0 resolves one target per suite in `tests/db/localPostgres.harness.mjs` (`resolveDisposableTarget`): the supplied DSN is validated and never repaired (a socket directory strictly below the temp root, in normal form, and still below it once the symlinks of its nearest existing ancestor resolve — a dangling link is refused; no port; unknown DSN parameters refused), and otherwise a fresh socket-only cluster is started. Every client is built by `testClient` / `testDriver` / `testDriverVia` / `checkedDriver` with host, port, role, database and password explicit (the password a function, so an empty one cannot fall back), and each refuses while any ambient database setting is present. A driver call must name its role: a socket URL has no host, so a username set on it is silently dropped. Six converted call sites (productionEntry 2, productionEntryPreflight 2, tenantAdmission 1, tenantDirectory 1) had lost their explicit role and ran as the target's owner; the first final run failed M6PF-01 (least-privilege preflight) and M5T-10 (revoked grant → 503), while productionEntry and tenantAdmission passed with those clients as the owner. The role is explicit again at every site, and `driverOver` refuses a call that names none (DBISO-4). The harness removes every `PG*`, `…DATABASE…` and `…DB_URL` variable from its process on load; `scripts/run-db-tests.mjs` (now `npm run test:db` and every CI database step) and every suite subprocess get an allowlisted environment (`childEnv`). The test executor (`createPostgresExecutor`) now passes port and password explicitly and refuses a DSN that names no role. Guarded by `tests/quality/db-test-ambient-fallback.test.mjs` (runner) and `db-test-ambient-fallback.suites.mjs` (its own CI step): a synthetic ambient configuration points at a listener the test owns, and the entry code dialed it 21/2/2 times (migrationEngine / rlsIsolation / auditAtomicity) and 28 times through `npm run test:db`; the fix dials it 0 times. **Residuals:** (1) a TCP target is accepted only at `127.0.0.1` with an explicit port, on GitHub Actions, when the step declares `TM_POS_TEST_DATABASE_KIND=ci-disposable-service` — a deliberate, non-ambient declaration, but the client cannot prove the listener behind it is the job's disposable service; (2) `scripts/supabase-migrate.ts --status` reaches the executor without the harness, so a supplied socket directory there is checked lexically (normal form, strictly below the temp root) but not through symlinks; (3) `checkedDriver` (only the driver-defect probe child) accepts the probe's own `socket` connection hook without a transport check, by design — its other fields stay explicit and ambient settings are still refused | M7A-DBTEST-ISOLATION-P0 | Closed for ambient fallback; residuals (1)–(3) accepted for test infrastructure; G-MIGRATE |
| DA-110 | **The driver's type fetch could reject unhandled when the transaction kernel retired a fresh pool, and the runtime treats that as fatal (closed for the kernel, M6R-PERF-R1).** CI run 36354123120 (attempt 1, `9a25cb7`) failed M6R-PERF (`tests/db/rateLimiter.integration.test.mjs`) with `unhandledRejection write CONNECTION_DESTROYED`. The same signature failed attempt 1 of CI 36152536918 (`7813bd1`) and of CI 36232237438 (`a4328974`), both before M7A-DBTEST-ISOLATION-P0; a code-path analysis found that P0 changed nothing that affects it. The mechanism, from matching stack frames and driver source: postgres 3.4.9, with `fetch_types` at its default of true, runs an untagged array-type query on a fresh connection's first ReadyForQuery and discards its promise (`connection.js`, `fetchArrayTypes`). The kernel retires a pool it distrusts with `end({ timeout: 0 })` (`server/persistence/supervisedPgClient.ts`), which a deadline abort such as the limiter's 500 ms bound (`LIMITER_DEADLINE_MS`) reaches. If that destroy lands during the one type-fetch round trip, the type query's rejection has no handler. The production kernel's pools used the default `fetch_types` (`server/platform-identity/db.ts`), and the runtime lifecycle ends the process on any unhandled rejection (`fatal_unhandled_rejection`, `server/runtime/lifecycle.ts`), so a deployed process could exit on the same timing. It is the same class as DA-15 and related to DA-60's load sensitivity. A green rerun is not a repair. *(M6R-PERF-R1, 2026-10-10: reproduced on the entry code by withholding, at the suite's relay, the reply to a fresh connection's first query and ending its transaction — the CI signature and stack — and corrected in the kernel: `fetch_types: false` forced beside `prepare: false`, after an audit found no statement sent through the kernel that binds or returns an array type; RLR-06 fails with it reverted.)* | M7A-DBTEST-ISOLATION-P0 CI (recorded M7A-CUSTOMERS-P3) | **Closed in code for the transaction kernel** (M6R-PERF-R1, commit `71bbb03`; RLR-06 and the kernel's option pin). Open outside it: every other postgres.js client keeps the driver default and sends the type query on each new connection — the platform's admin and runtime pools (`server/platform-identity/db.ts`, `idle_timeout` 20 s, so new connections open while requests are served), the migration executor's two clients (a frozen path) and the identity-validation script's client. In the driver's code, a connection failure of any kind during that query — not only a retirement — rejects it with no handler (read in the code, not reproduced for these clients); that these clients are retired only at disposal or shutdown does not exclude it. The deployable entry composes only the kernel (wording corrected in M6R-PERF-R2). G-IDEMPOT, with DA-15 |
| DA-111 | **The customer create's audit row does not name the customer.** The row records who (the app-owned actor), where (scope `tenant` and the tenant), what (`tenant.customer.create`) and under which permission, with `request_id` the command's server-generated correlation id and metadata `{}`. Nothing links it to the created customer's id: `subject_tenant_id` names tenant aggregates only, the writer's metadata allow-list has no customer key, and the command enqueues no outbox event sharing its correlation id. DA-22 had the same gap for a tenant target, closed by 009's column. The name is deliberately never written to audit *(M7A-CUSTOMERS-P7, [ADR-42](./10-architecture-decision-records.md): the customer EDIT's record names its customer in the new typed column `subject_customer_id` (migration 024). The create's record is unchanged and still names none, so this row stays open for the create.)* | M7A-CUSTOMERS-P3 | G-AUDIT: the create also setting the typed `subject_customer_id` column that migration 024 added for the edit (no new column needed) |
| DA-112 | **Every tenant instance must hold one `IDEMPOTENCY_KEY`, and nothing checks it.** A record's scope is an HMAC under the process's key. Two tenant instances with different keys therefore both acquire the same client key, and can create two customers for one request. The tenant entry checks only that its own key is present, canonical and different from `RATE_LIMIT_KEY` and its issuer key; whether it equals its peers' cannot be judged by one process. The administrative boundary has the same gap (G-IDEMPOT) | M7A-CUSTOMERS-P3 | Deployment condition (G-IDEMPOT). A registered key fingerprint, as 014 keeps for `RATE_LIMIT_KEY`, is a later option |
| DA-113 | **READ COMMITTED is a deployment condition for the customer create.** 019's routine takes its locks, then decides on statements whose snapshots are taken after the lock waits, which is sound only under READ COMMITTED. Under any other isolation it raises 55000 and writes nothing. The kernel sets no isolation level, so a database or login whose default transaction isolation is not READ COMMITTED refuses every create (503). Readiness stays green meanwhile: no startup or readiness check reads the setting *(M7A-CUSTOMERS-P7, [ADR-42](./10-architecture-decision-records.md): 024's `tmpos_customer_edit.m7a_customer_update` makes the same demand: under any other isolation it raises 55000 and writes nothing, so such a database or login refuses every customer edit too (503).)* | M7A-CUSTOMERS-P3 | Deployment condition, a G-DBROLE provisioning check. No preflight check was added |
| DA-114 | **A tenant-scope audit row's provenance is TypeScript-only.** Migration 005's append policy checks a row's shape, not the session's context, so the database does not prove that a tenant-scope row's tenant and actor are the command's. The store proves them equal to the revalidated trusted context. The customer create's mutator proves that context equal to the tenant, actor and grant 019 derived from the session. A holder of the runtime login can still insert a well-shaped tenant-scope audit row directly | M7A-CUSTOMERS-P3 | G-AUDIT, with G-DBROLE |
| DA-115 | **019's residuals for a runtime-role holder with a live Create-capable session key.** (1) It chooses the customer id. The 201, id included, is sealed into the completion before COMMIT, so the runtime supplies a version-4 id and the routine accepts any well-formed one. (2) The primary key is global (017), so a 23505 tells it whether an id it chooses exists in any tenant. Random 122-bit ids limit this to confirming ids it already knows. (3) After the pre-check, any live key minted under the installed issuer key — whatever its role or statuses (a technician's, a `read_only` or `overdue` tenant's included) — still takes 016's hold, the session and ledger rows and the issuer-key row before it can be refused. A holder of `tmpos_app` can keep that transaction open indefinitely, not merely until the session expires: the database ends no transaction at session expiry, and a raw session under the role can set its own `idle_in_transaction_session_timeout`. While it stays open it delays that principal's logins and commands, standing and grant changes (the account row FOR UPDATE); 010's status command on, and any update of, every tenant and store that principal's grants name (FOR SHARE); touches, logout and revocation of the held session row (not of the principal's other sessions); every issuer-key rotation; and, once the held ledger row is itself a day past its expiry, 018's prune (run by every tenant login), so every tenant login fails until the holder's transaction ends — the same class as that holder exhausting connections. The account, tenant and store delays it can already cause with 016's hold alone (DA-82). The HTTP path cannot do this: the tenant revalidator refuses a session that is not live before 019 ever runs (`server/composition/tenantAuthorization.ts`, the session read at decision 4), and every command transaction it opens is itself bounded, `idle_in_transaction_session_timeout` included (`server/persistence/postgresTransactionalStore.ts`'s `boundsUnder`, applied per transaction by `supervisedPgClient.ts`); only a raw session held outside that path, under the bare runtime role, can hold one open past those bounds. Not a residual: an owner-installed BEFORE INSERT trigger on the customer table that returns NULL cannot make a create commit without its row, because 019 refuses when its INSERT wrote no row (`no_data_found`), so the transaction rolls back and the client gets 503; only the table owner can install such a trigger. *(M7A-CUSTOMERS-P3-R1: `tmpos_app` no longer executes 019's routine; these residuals carry to 020's `tmpos_role.m7a_customer_create`, now judged on the owner's stored level (DA-118), and 020's role row adds a lock the same holder can keep (DA-120).)* | M7A-CUSTOMERS-P3 | G-DBROLE, with DA-82 and DA-97 |
| DA-116 | **Rolling back 019.** Its down file drops only its routine and schema. It does not refuse while customers exist: they are 017's rows and stay readable. Every `POST /api/v1/customers` then answers 503. Both entries' startup preflight pins 019's ledger row and its two grants, so a restart of either, the administrative entry included, refuses (`role_privilege_missing`) until the code rolls back too. A PL/pgSQL body records no dependency, so 016's, 017's or 018's rollback succeeds while 019 is applied, and the routine then raises (failing closed) until the chain is whole | M7A-CUSTOMERS-P3 | G-MIGRATE: roll 019 back first, together with the code |
| DA-117 | **Two chain behaviours the first tenant command inherits.** (1) A repeated JSON key keeps its last value (`JSON.parse`), on every body route; the fingerprint binds the exact bytes sent. A corrected body under the same key is then a 422 while the record is retained (24 h), so the client must use a new key | M7A-CUSTOMERS-P3 review | Recorded, not changed: both apply to every command |
